LAMBADA ($\\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category.}\n\\label{fig:task_specificity}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-architecture convergence across benchmarks (1.3B scale). $\\rho$: Spearman correlation between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\\sigma$ across five configurations. $p$: exact permutation test (two-tailed, 120 permutations). $\\sigma$ values reported $\\times 10^{2}$ (accuracy percentage points). Ratio: $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}} / \\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$. \\textbf{Bold}: only benchmark with $\\rho = -1.0$.}\n\\label{tab:convergence}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Category & $\\rho$ & $p$ & $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}}$ & $\\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$ & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{LAMBADA$^{\\ddagger}$} & \\textbf{Retrieval} & $\\mathbf{-1.0}$ & \\textbf{.017} & \\textbf{2.41} & \\textbf{0.49} & \\textbf{4.87} \\\\\nBoolQ & Comprehension & $0.2$ & .783 & 1.63 & 2.36 & 0.69 \\\\\nARC-C & Reasoning & $0.6$ & .350 & 0.96 & 1.32 & 0.72 \\\\\nHellaSwag & Reasoning & $0.4$ & .517 & 1.17 & 1.22 & 0.96 \\\\\nPIQA & Reasoning & $-0.5$ & .450 & 0.54 & 0.34 & 1.59 \\\\\nWinoGrande & Reasoning & $0.1$ & .950 & 0.67 & 0.78 & 0.86 \\\\\nARC-E & Mixed & $-0.7$ & .233 & 1.00 & 0.13 & 7.70 \\\\\nMMLU & Mixed & $-0.7$ & .233 & 0.92 & 0.41 & 2.27 \\\\\nSciQ & Mixed & $0.1$ & .950 & 0.87 & 0.74 & 1.19 \\\\\nXStoryCloze & Mixed & $-0.6$ & .350 & 0.82 & 0.55 & 1.49 \\\\\n\\midrule\nCOPA$^{\\dagger}$ & Reasoning & $-0.67$ & .267 & 2.63 & 0.47 & 5.57 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^{\\dagger}$Supplementary ($n = 100$); excluded from multiple comparison correction.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^{\\ddagger}$GDN h3-1 uses 16 attention heads; all other configurations use 32 (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}).} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize Bonferroni-adjusted $p = 6 \\times 2/120 = 0.100$ (exact); 0.102 arises from rounding raw $p$ to .017 before multiplying.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\\label{ssec:per_example}\n\nDo architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped (correct only in hybrid), hurt (correct only in pure), or unchanged. Although the decomposition is binary, we verify below that it captures genuine separation in the continuous confidence space (Cohen's $d = 0.73$--$0.92$ between helped and hurt on LAMBADA; cross-benchmark details in Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}). This binarization discards confidence magnitude---a transition from 0.51 to 0.49 is treated identically to one from 0.99 to 0.01---so we supplement it with continuous confidence margin analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}).\n\nEach architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Table~\\ref{tab:per_example}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which attention produces a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\\bar{J} \\leq 0.104$ for helped, $\\leq 0.096$ for hurt, indicating ${\\sim}90$\\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\\text{null}} \\approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), but normalizing by the theoretical upper bound $J_{\\max} = \\min(|A|,|B|)/\\max(|A|,|B|)$, observed overlap reaches only 5--7\\% of the achievable range above chance (normalized ES 0.065 helped, 0.047 hurt; per-pair details in Appendix~\\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.\n\nNo surface-level feature (token count, word frequency, syntactic complexity) reliably separates helped from hurt examples. However, pure-model confidence on the target token is strongly predictive within each architecture: hurt examples have lower target-token probability among pure-correct examples ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$), and helped examples have higher probability among pure-wrong examples ($d = 0.73$--$0.77$); see Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}. A Kruskal-Wallis test confirms cross-architecture structure in both hurt ($p = .0005$) and helped ($p = .020$) distributions. The confidence margin thus identifies a per-example predictor of which examples will be affected: attention disrupts marginally correct predictions and rescues near-miss errors, but with architecture-specific thresholds that produce the observed cross-architecture divergence. The rescue pattern generalizes: across all three architectures on LAMBADA, HellaSwag, and ARC-Easy, helped examples have significantly higher pure-model target probability than stayed-wrong examples (9/9 tests, $d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}). The disruption pattern is weaker on ARC-Easy ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$), absent on HellaSwag under \\texttt{acc\\_norm}, and absent on binary classification (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}), likely reflecting structural properties of constrained-choice formats that limit the discriminative power of confidence margins. This structured per-example disagreement despite similar aggregate performance constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} arising from architectural variation rather than random initialization \\citep{damour2022underspecification}.\n\n\\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent variation. Evaluation format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way, $\\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (two-choice, $\\alpha = 0.14$) produce nearly identical multiplicity despite different formats; among two-choice benchmarks alone, $\\alpha$ spans $3\\times$ from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.41 (WinoGrande; Appendix~\\ref{app:winogrande}) to 0.45 (BoolQ; Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}), covering the full six-benchmark range within a single format. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm the ordering: all adjacent-benchmark CIs are non-overlapping except PIQA and HellaSwag, whose CIs overlap substantially ($[.127,\\,.159]$ vs.\\ $[.141,\\,.155]$; Appendix, Table~\\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \\citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}. The near-identical $\\alpha$ for PIQA (0.14) and HellaSwag (0.15) despite different evaluation formats suggests a shared reasoning-task floor for multiplicity, while retrieval (LAMBADA, 0.30) and mixed (ARC-Easy, 0.21) tasks produce distinctly higher values.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Predictive multiplicity at 1.3B. $\\alpha$: ambiguity at h3-1 \\citep{marx2020predictive}. $\\bar{J}$: mean pairwise Jaccard of helped~(h) and hurt~(u) sets, averaged across four hybrid attention ratios and three architecture pairs. $\\rho_\\alpha$: Spearman $\\rho$ between attention ratio and $\\alpha$. Benchmarks ordered by $\\alpha$.}\n\\label{tab:multiplicity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n & $N$ & $\\alpha$ & $\\bar{J}_{h}$ & $\\bar{J}_{u}$ & $\\rho_\\alpha$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPIQA & 1838 & .14 & .091 & .094 & $0.0$ \\\\\nHellaSwag & 10042 & .15 & .095 & .085 & $0.1$ \\\\\nARC-Easy & 2376 & .21 & .092 & .096 & $-0.2$ \\\\\nLAMBADA & 5153 & .30 & .086 & .091 & $-0.6$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\par\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\footnotesize Within-benchmark $\\alpha$ variation $< 0.03$ across ratios. BoolQ, WinoGrande: Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}, \\ref{app:winogrande}.}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Decoupling}\\label{ssec:decoupling}\n\nA key consequence of this multiplicity is that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence operate on different timescales as attention ratio increases (Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}).\n\nCross-architecture $\\sigma$ decreases gradually across the five attention ratios: $0.024 \\to 0.016 \\to 0.014 \\to 0.006 \\to 0.005$. In contrast, pairwise Jaccard similarity between architectures' per-example effects saturates at the first attention level (4\\%): approximately 0.095 for helped, with no further increase at higher ratios. Net asymmetry between architectures' per-example profiles increases from 0.013 at 4\\% attention to 0.040 at 25\\%. If convergence arose from attention uniformly rescuing the same difficult examples across architectures, Jaccard would increase in parallel with decreasing $\\sigma$. The observed saturation of Jaccard at the first attention level indicates that each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\\bar{J} \\approx 0.10$ is far below the theoretical maximum $J_{\\max} \\approx 0.78$--$0.92$ given helped-set sizes, ruling out ceiling constraints: there is ample room for Jaccard to increase if attention were homogenizing per-example behavior (normalized effect size 5--7\\% of achievable range; \\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}).\n\nWe operationalize this separation as decoupling when two conditions hold jointly: (i)~aggregate convergence, $\\rho(\\mathrm{rank}(r),\\,\\sigma(r)) = -1.0$; and (ii)~per-example saturation, $\\mathrm{CI}[\\bar{J}(r)] \\cap \\mathrm{CI}[\\bar{J}(r')] \\neq \\emptyset$ for all $r, r' > 0$. 1	\documentclass[11pt]{article}

17	\title{Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Linear Recurrent Architectures}
18	
19	\author{Anonymous}
20	
21	\begin{document}
22	\maketitle
23	
24	\begin{abstract}
25	Hybrid linear recurrent architectures that interleave attention with recurrence are increasingly deployed, yet how attention affects per-example behavior across backbones remains unquantified. Using the controlled model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}---three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales (340M/1.3B)---we evaluate 10 benchmarks and decompose per-example effects on four (LAMBADA, HellaSwag, PIQA, ARC-Easy). Per-example predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} varies across tasks ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) while pairwise Jaccard of helped/hurt sets remains uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$); multiplicity magnitude depends on task type, not attention dose. Aggregate convergence, by contrast, is task-specific: only LAMBADA provides suggestive evidence for monotonic variance reduction (79\%; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$), consistent with attention compensating for retrieval differences across recurrent backbones. On LAMBADA, aggregate variance decreases gradually while per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level---the two are decoupled.
26	\end{abstract}
27	
28	\input{sections/introduction}
29	
30	\input{sections/related_work}
31	
32	\input{sections/method}
33	
34	\input{sections/experiments}
35	
36	\input{sections/conclusion}
37	
38	\section*{Limitations}
39	
40	Our analysis has several limitations.
41	
42	\paragraph{Coverage.} Per-example analysis covers four main benchmarks; BoolQ and WinoGrande are supplementary (Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}, \ref{app:winogrande}). The correlation between $\alpha$ and task accuracy is moderate but not significant ($\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$); HellaSwag (60\%, $\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (73\%, $\alpha = 0.14$) show low $\alpha$ despite varied accuracy, while benchmarks near chance level (BoolQ, WinoGrande) show the highest $\alpha$. Accuracy alone does not explain the variation, though we cannot fully rule out more complex interactions. We examine three recurrent architectures from one model zoo; the controlled design (shared training data, tokenizer, hyperparameters) isolates architectural effects but limits generalization to other hybrid families.
43	
44	\paragraph{Methodological constraints.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable two-tailed $p$-value is approximately 0.017, contributing to the Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ for LAMBADA not reaching conventional significance. Each zoo configuration corresponds to a single training run; we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects, though the $3\times$ Jaccard gap (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}) provides proxy evidence for architecture as the dominant factor. Consequently, all cross-architecture per-example claims are conditioned on single training runs per configuration. At 340M, $\alpha$ is systematically higher than at 1.3B (by up to 8.4pp on ARC-Easy), but pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$), indicating that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity is more robust than its absolute magnitude. Our attention weight analysis is descriptive, not causal; we compare only two scale points (340M, 1.3B)---a binary observation, not a scaling law.
45	
46	\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All models are evaluated after pre-training without fine-tuning; per-example effects and the decoupling pattern may not persist after task-specific adaptation. We evaluate only discrete accuracy; continuous metrics such as log-probability or calibrated confidence may reveal different patterns of multiplicity.
47	
48	\section*{Ethics Statement}
49	
50	This work analyzes publicly available pretrained language models from the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}. No new data collection, human subjects research, or model training is involved. We identify per-example behavioral differences across architectures, which may inform practitioners about the limitations of aggregate benchmark comparisons but does not introduce new risks.
51	
52	\bibliography{references}
53	
54	\appendix
55	\input{sections/appendix}
56	
57	\end{document}

Using the controlled model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}---three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales (340M/1.3B)---we evaluate 10 benchmarks and decompose per-example effects on four (LAMBADA, HellaSwag, PIQA, ARC-Easy). Per-example predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} varies across tasks ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) while pairwise Jaccard of helped/hurt sets remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$); multiplicity magnitude depends on task type, not attention dose. Aggregate convergence, by contrast, is task-specific: only LAMBADA provides suggestive evidence for monotonic variance reduction (79\\%; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$), consistent with attention compensating for retrieval differences across recurrent backbones. On LAMBADA, aggregate variance decreases gradually while per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level---the two are decoupled.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{sections/introduction}\n\n\\input{sections/related_work}\n\n\\input{sections/method}\n\n\\input{sections/experiments}\n\n\\input{sections/conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur analysis has several limitations.\n\n\\paragraph{Coverage.} Per-example analysis covers four main benchmarks; BoolQ and WinoGrande are supplementary (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}, \\ref{app:winogrande}). The correlation between $\\alpha$ and task accuracy is moderate but not significant ($\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$); HellaSwag (60\\%, $\\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (73\\%, $\\alpha = 0.14$) show low $\\alpha$ despite varied accuracy, while benchmarks near chance level (BoolQ, WinoGrande) show the highest $\\alpha$. Accuracy alone does not explain the variation, though we cannot fully rule out more complex interactions. We examine three recurrent architectures from one model zoo; the controlled design (shared training data, tokenizer, hyperparameters) isolates architectural effects but limits generalization to other hybrid families.\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological constraints.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable two-tailed $p$-value is approximately 0.017, contributing to the Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ for LAMBADA not reaching conventional significance. Each zoo configuration corresponds to a single training run; we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects, though the $3\\times$ Jaccard gap (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) provides proxy evidence for architecture as the dominant factor. Consequently, all cross-architecture per-example claims are conditioned on single training runs per configuration. At 340M, $\\alpha$ is systematically higher than at 1.3B (by up to 8.4pp on ARC-Easy), but pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$), indicating that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity is more robust than its absolute magnitude. Our attention weight analysis is descriptive, not causal; we compare only two scale points (340M, 1.3B)---a binary observation, not a scaling law.\n\n\\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All models are evaluated after pre-training without fine-tuning; per-example effects and the decoupling pattern may not persist after task-specific adaptation. We evaluate only discrete accuracy; continuous metrics such as log-probability or calibrated confidence may reveal different patterns of multiplicity.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work analyzes publicly available pretrained language models from the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. No new data collection, human subjects research, or model training is involved. We identify per-example behavioral differences across architectures, which may inform practitioners about the limitations of aggregate benchmark comparisons but does not introduce new risks.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{sections/appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

    }

    "newString": "Our analysis yields two findings.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nWhen attention layers are interleaved into recurrent architectures, they help and hurt almost entirely different examples---pairwise Jaccard overlap among three hybrid designs is at most 0.10, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by rescuing and disrupting different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores; architectures that score identically in aggregate may thus carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \\citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \\citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks---LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$), PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$), and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$)---we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields three findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent variation. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval). Second, attention-driven aggregate convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic given $n = 3$, \\S\\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is present at 1.3B but absent at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. The experiments that follow begin with convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}) because it establishes the aggregate backdrop against which per-example divergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}) and decoupling (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) are measured.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We show that architectural variation---under matched training conditions---produces structured per-example predictive multiplicity with a task-dependent variation ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks in three recurrent backbones, with magnitude independent of aggregate convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Unlike seed-driven disagreement within a single architecture \\citep{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, this multiplicity is not addressable by seed averaging: the source of variation is the recurrent backbone rather than random initialization, though we cannot fully disentangle the two with single training runs (Limitations).\n    \\item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence, and this pattern is scale-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}; \\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}).\n    \\item We demonstrate that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled: cross-architecture $\\sigma$ decreases monotonically with attention ratio while pairwise Jaccard saturates at the first attention level, formally operationalized via confidence-interval overlap conditions (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

Using the controlled model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}---three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales (340M/1.3B)---we evaluate 10 benchmarks and decompose per-example effects on four (LAMBADA, HellaSwag, PIQA, ARC-Easy). Per-example predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} varies across tasks ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) while pairwise Jaccard of helped/hurt sets remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$); multiplicity magnitude depends on task type, not attention dose. Aggregate convergence, by contrast, is task-specific: only LAMBADA provides suggestive evidence for monotonic variance reduction (79\\%; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$), consistent with attention compensating for retrieval differences across recurrent backbones. On LAMBADA, aggregate variance decreases gradually while per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level---the two are decoupled.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{sections/introduction}\n\n\\input{sections/related_work}\n\n\\input{sections/method}\n\n\\input{sections/experiments}\n\n\\input{sections/conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur analysis has several limitations.\n\n\\paragraph{Coverage.} Per-example analysis covers four main benchmarks; BoolQ and WinoGrande are supplementary (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}, \\ref{app:winogrande}). The correlation between $\\alpha$ and task accuracy is moderate but not significant ($\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$); HellaSwag (60\\%, $\\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (73\\%, $\\alpha = 0.14$) show low $\\alpha$ despite varied accuracy, while benchmarks near chance level (BoolQ, WinoGrande) show the highest $\\alpha$. Accuracy alone does not explain the variation, though we cannot fully rule out more complex interactions. We examine three recurrent architectures from one model zoo; the controlled design (shared training data, tokenizer, hyperparameters) isolates architectural effects but limits generalization to other hybrid families.\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological constraints.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable two-tailed $p$-value is approximately 0.017, contributing to the Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ for LAMBADA not reaching conventional significance. Each zoo configuration corresponds to a single training run; we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects, though the $3\\times$ Jaccard gap (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) provides proxy evidence for architecture as the dominant factor. Consequently, all cross-architecture per-example claims are conditioned on single training runs per configuration. At 340M, $\\alpha$ is systematically higher than at 1.3B (by up to 8.4pp on ARC-Easy), but pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$), indicating that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity is more robust than its absolute magnitude. Our attention weight analysis is descriptive, not causal; we compare only two scale points (340M, 1.3B)---a binary observation, not a scaling law.\n\n\\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All models are evaluated after pre-training without fine-tuning; per-example effects and the decoupling pattern may not persist after task-specific adaptation. We evaluate only discrete accuracy; continuous metrics such as log-probability or calibrated confidence may reveal different patterns of multiplicity.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work analyzes publicly available pretrained language models from the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. No new data collection, human subjects research, or model training is involved. We identify per-example behavioral differences across architectures, which may inform practitioners about the limitations of aggregate benchmark comparisons but does not introduce new risks.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{sections/appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of attention effects in hybrid recurrent models. Per-example multiplicity spans a task-dependent variation ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with Jaccard uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$) regardless of aggregate convergence. Aggregate convergence, by contrast, is task-specific (only LAMBADA) and present at 1.3B but absent at 340M.\n\nFor practitioners selecting among hybrid architectures, these results suggest a concrete protocol. Per-example audits are warranted whenever multiple hybrid designs achieve comparable aggregate scores on a target task: the low Jaccard ($\\leq 0.10$, indicating ${\\sim}90\\%$ non-overlap) means that architectures which appear interchangeable by aggregate metrics affect nearly disjoint sets of examples. In deployment contexts where prediction consistency matters---medical, legal, or safety-critical applications---aggregate equivalence does not imply per-example equivalence, and architecture choice may determine which specific inputs are handled correctly. A proof-of-concept majority vote across three architectures does not outperform the best individual architecture (LAMBADA: $-0.2$\\,pp; ARC-Easy: $-1.1$\\,pp), despite 15--45\\% per-example disagreement. Oracle accuracy---correct if any architecture is correct---reveals substantial complementarity potential ($+7$--$14$\\,pp above best), but majority vote captures only 1--9\\% of this ceiling. This gap quantifies the structural cost of multiplicity: when two of three architectures agree on the wrong answer, the correct minority prediction is inaccessible to naive ensembling, motivating future work on architecture-aware selection methods.\n",

Per-example multiplicity spans a task-dependent variation ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with Jaccard uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$) regardless of aggregate convergence. On LAMBADA, aggregate variance decreases with attention while per-example overlap saturates, suggesting architecture-specific mechanisms rather than uniform rescue; this observation is dependent on GDN inclusion and does not replicate on other benchmarks.\n\nFor practitioners selecting among hybrid architectures, these results suggest a concrete protocol. Per-example audits are warranted whenever multiple hybrid designs achieve comparable aggregate scores on a target task: the low Jaccard ($\\leq 0.10$, indicating ${\\sim}90\\%$ non-overlap) means that architectures which appear interchangeable by aggregate metrics affect nearly disjoint sets of examples. In deployment contexts where prediction consistency matters---medical, legal, or safety-critical applications---aggregate equivalence does not imply per-example equivalence, and architecture choice may determine which specific inputs are handled correctly. A proof-of-concept majority vote across three architectures does not outperform the best individual architecture (LAMBADA: $-0.2$\\,pp; ARC-Easy: $-1.1$\\,pp), despite 15--45\\% per-example disagreement. Oracle accuracy---correct if any architecture is correct---reveals substantial complementarity potential ($+7$--$14$\\,pp above best), but majority vote captures only 1--9\\% of this ceiling. This gap quantifies the structural cost of multiplicity: when two of three architectures agree on the wrong answer, the correct minority prediction is inaccessible to naive ensembling, motivating future work on architecture-aware selection methods.\n",

In deployment contexts where prediction consistency matters---medical, legal, or safety-critical applications---aggregate equivalence does not imply per-example equivalence, and architecture choice may determine which specific inputs are handled correctly.     "oldString": "Second, attention-driven aggregate convergence",
    "newString": "Attention-driven aggregate convergence",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nWhen attention layers are interleaved into recurrent architectures, they help and hurt almost entirely different examples---pairwise Jaccard overlap among three hybrid designs is at most 0.10, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by rescuing and disrupting different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores; architectures that score identically in aggregate may thus carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \\citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \\citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks---LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$), PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$), and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$)---we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent variation. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval). Second, attention-driven aggregate convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic given $n = 3$, \\S\\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is present at 1.3B but absent at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. The experiments that follow begin with convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}) because it establishes the aggregate backdrop against which per-example divergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}) and decoupling (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) are measured.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We show that architectural variation---under matched training conditions---produces structured per-example predictive multiplicity with a task-dependent variation ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks in three recurrent backbones, with magnitude independent of aggregate convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Unlike seed-driven disagreement within a single architecture \\citep{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, this multiplicity is not addressable by seed averaging: the source of variation is the recurrent backbone rather than random initialization, though we cannot fully disentangle the two with single training runs (Limitations).\n    \\item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence, and this pattern is scale-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}; \\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}).\n    \\item We demonstrate that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled: cross-architecture $\\sigma$ decreases monotonically with attention ratio while pairwise Jaccard saturates at the first attention level, formally operationalized via confidence-interval overlap conditions (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{amssymb}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\n\\title{Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Linear Recurrent Architectures}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nHybrid linear recurrent architectures that interleave attention with recurrence are increasingly deployed, yet how attention affects per-example behavior across backbones remains unquantified. Using the controlled model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}---three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales (340M/1.3B)---we evaluate 10 benchmarks and decompose per-example effects on four (LAMBADA, HellaSwag, PIQA, ARC-Easy). Per-example predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} varies across tasks ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) while pairwise Jaccard of helped/hurt sets remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$); multiplicity magnitude depends on task type, not attention dose. Aggregate convergence, by contrast, is task-specific: only LAMBADA provides suggestive evidence for monotonic variance reduction (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$), consistent with attention compensating for retrieval differences across recurrent backbones. On LAMBADA, aggregate variance decreases gradually while per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level---the two are decoupled.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{sections/introduction}\n\n\\input{sections/related_work}\n\n\\input{sections/method}\n\n\\input{sections/experiments}\n\n\\input{sections/conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur analysis has several limitations.\n\n\\paragraph{Coverage.} Per-example analysis covers four main benchmarks; BoolQ and WinoGrande are supplementary (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}, \\ref{app:winogrande}). The correlation between $\\alpha$ and task accuracy is moderate but not significant ($\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$); HellaSwag (60\\%, $\\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (73\\%, $\\alpha = 0.14$) show low $\\alpha$ despite varied accuracy, while benchmarks near chance level (BoolQ, WinoGrande) show the highest $\\alpha$. Accuracy alone does not explain the variation, though we cannot fully rule out more complex interactions. We examine three recurrent architectures from one model zoo; the controlled design (shared training data, tokenizer, hyperparameters) isolates architectural effects but limits generalization to other hybrid families.\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological constraints.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable two-tailed $p$-value is approximately 0.017, contributing to the Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ for LAMBADA not reaching conventional significance. Each zoo configuration corresponds to a single training run; we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects, though the $3\\times$ Jaccard gap (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) provides proxy evidence for architecture as the dominant factor. Consequently, all cross-architecture per-example claims are conditioned on single training runs per configuration. At 340M, $\\alpha$ is systematically higher than at 1.3B (by up to 8.4pp on ARC-Easy), but pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$), indicating that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity is more robust than its absolute magnitude. Our attention weight analysis is descriptive, not causal; we compare only two scale points (340M, 1.3B)---a binary observation, not a scaling law.\n\n\\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All models are evaluated after pre-training without fine-tuning; per-example effects and the decoupling pattern may not persist after task-specific adaptation. We evaluate only discrete accuracy; continuous metrics such as log-probability or calibrated confidence may reveal different patterns of multiplicity.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work analyzes publicly available pretrained language models from the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. No new data collection, human subjects research, or model training is involved. We identify per-example behavioral differences across architectures, which may inform practitioners about the limitations of aggregate benchmark comparisons but does not introduce new risks.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{sections/appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nWhen attention layers are interleaved into recurrent architectures, they help and hurt almost entirely different examples---pairwise Jaccard overlap among three hybrid designs is at most 0.10, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by rescuing and disrupting different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores; architectures that score identically in aggregate may thus carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \\citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \\citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks---LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$), PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$), and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$)---we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent variation. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval). Attention-driven aggregate convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic given $n = 3$, \\S\\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is present at 1.3B but absent at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. The experiments that follow begin with convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}) because it establishes the aggregate backdrop against which per-example divergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}) and decoupling (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) are measured.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We show that architectural variation---under matched training conditions---produces structured per-example predictive multiplicity with a task-dependent variation ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks in three recurrent backbones, with magnitude independent of aggregate convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Unlike seed-driven disagreement within a single architecture \\citep{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, this multiplicity is not addressable by seed averaging: the source of variation is the recurrent backbone rather than random initialization, though we cannot fully disentangle the two with single training runs (Limitations).\n    \\item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence, and this pattern is scale-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}; \\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}).\n    \\item We demonstrate that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled: cross-architecture $\\sigma$ decreases monotonically with attention ratio while pairwise Jaccard saturates at the first attention level, formally operationalized via confidence-interval overlap conditions (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{amssymb}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\n\\title{Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Linear Recurrent Architectures}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nHybrid linear recurrent architectures that interleave attention with recurrence are increasingly deployed, yet how attention affects per-example behavior across backbones remains unquantified. Using the controlled model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}---three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales (340M/1.3B), single training runs per configuration---we evaluate 10 benchmarks and decompose per-example effects on four (LAMBADA, HellaSwag, PIQA, ARC-Easy). Per-example predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} varies across tasks ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) while pairwise Jaccard of helped/hurt sets remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$); multiplicity magnitude depends on task type, not attention dose. Aggregate convergence, by contrast, is task-specific: only LAMBADA provides suggestive evidence for monotonic variance reduction (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$), consistent with attention compensating for retrieval differences across recurrent backbones. On LAMBADA, aggregate variance decreases gradually while per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level---the two are decoupled.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{sections/introduction}\n\n\\input{sections/related_work}\n\n\\input{sections/method}\n\n\\input{sections/experiments}\n\n\\input{sections/conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur analysis has several limitations.\n\n\\paragraph{Coverage.} Per-example analysis covers four main benchmarks; BoolQ and WinoGrande are supplementary (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}, \\ref{app:winogrande}). The correlation between $\\alpha$ and task accuracy is moderate but not significant ($\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$); HellaSwag (60\\%, $\\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (73\\%, $\\alpha = 0.14$) show low $\\alpha$ despite varied accuracy, while benchmarks near chance level (BoolQ, WinoGrande) show the highest $\\alpha$. Accuracy alone does not explain the variation, though we cannot fully rule out more complex interactions. We examine three recurrent architectures from one model zoo; the controlled design (shared training data, tokenizer, hyperparameters) isolates architectural effects but limits generalization to other hybrid families.\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological constraints.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable two-tailed $p$-value is approximately 0.017, contributing to the Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ for LAMBADA not reaching conventional significance. Each zoo configuration corresponds to a single training run; we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects, though the $3\\times$ Jaccard gap (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) provides proxy evidence for architecture as the dominant factor. The within-architecture Jaccard (${\\sim}0.33$) compares models at different attention ratios, not different random seeds; it therefore provides a proxy for, not a direct measurement of, architecture-versus-seed effects. Consequently, all cross-architecture per-example claims are conditioned on single training runs per configuration. At 340M, $\\alpha$ is systematically higher than at 1.3B (by up to 8.4pp on ARC-Easy), but pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$), indicating that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity is more robust than its absolute magnitude. Our attention weight analysis is descriptive, not causal; we compare only two scale points (340M, 1.3B)---a binary observation, not a scaling law.\n\n\\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All models are evaluated after pre-training without fine-tuning; per-example effects and the decoupling pattern may not persist after task-specific adaptation. We evaluate only discrete accuracy; continuous metrics such as log-probability or calibrated confidence may reveal different patterns of multiplicity.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work analyzes publicly available pretrained language models from the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. No new data collection, human subjects research, or model training is involved. We identify per-example behavioral differences across architectures, which may inform practitioners about the limitations of aggregate benchmark comparisons but does not introduce new risks.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{sections/appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

440	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Extension}\label{app:cm_cross}
441	
442	The confidence margin pattern extends beyond LAMBADA to multiple-choice benchmarks, though with smaller effect sizes. Table~\ref{tab:cm_cross_c2} reports the rescue analysis (helped vs.\ stayed-wrong) on HellaSwag and ARC-Easy. All nine comparisons (three benchmarks $\times$ three architectures) are significant, confirming that attention consistently rescues near-miss errors regardless of task type. Effect sizes range from small on HellaSwag ($d = 0.13$--$0.17$) to medium on ARC-Easy ($d = 0.51$--$0.60$), compared to large on LAMBADA ($d = 0.73$--$0.77$); the attenuation on HellaSwag reflects noisier confidence proxies under length-normalized scoring. Table~\ref{tab:cm_cross_c1} reports the disruption analysis (hurt vs.\ stayed-right); two of six comparisons are significant (all on ARC-Easy) with small effect sizes ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$), indicating that the disruption signal is weaker on multiple-choice tasks and absent on HellaSwag under \texttt{acc\_norm}. The asymmetry between rescue and disruption reflects task structure: near-miss errors are an intrinsic sample property (the model almost produced the correct answer regardless of format), while borderline correctness in multiple-choice settings depends on relative option ranking rather than absolute confidence, reducing the raw log-likelihood margin's predictive power for vulnerability.
443	
444	\begin{table}[h]
445	\centering
446	\caption{Cross-benchmark rescue analysis (Control 2): helped vs.\ stayed-wrong on HellaSwag and ARC-Easy (1.3B h3-1).}
447	\label{tab:cm_cross_c2}
448	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
449	\begin{tabular}{llrr}
450	\toprule
451	Benchmark & Architecture & Cohen's $d$ & $p$ \\
452	\midrule
453	HellaSwag & GDN & $-$0.166 & $6.1 \times 10^{-4}$ \\
454	 & RetNet & $-$0.132 & $1.4 \times 10^{-2}$ \\
455	 & HGRN2 & $-$0.164 & $1.4 \times 10^{-3}$ \\
456	\midrule

Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\\text{null}} \\approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), but normalizing by the theoretical upper bound $J_{\\max} = \\min(|A|,|B|)/\\max(|A|,|B|)$, observed overlap reaches only 5--7\\% of the achievable range above chance (normalized ES 0.065 helped, 0.047 hurt; per-pair details in Appendix~\\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.",

A Kruskal-Wallis test confirms cross-architecture structure in both hurt ($p = .0005$) and helped ($p = .020$) distributions. The confidence margin thus identifies a per-example predictor of which examples will be affected: attention disrupts marginally correct predictions and rescues near-miss errors (we use `rescue' and `disruption' as descriptive labels for observed transitions, not as causal claims about attention mechanisms), but with architecture-specific thresholds that produce the observed cross-architecture divergence.           " \\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent variation. Evaluation format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way, $\\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (two-choice, $\\alpha = 0.14$) produce nearly identical multiplicity despite different formats; among two-choice benchmarks alone, $\\alpha$ spans $3\\times$ from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.41 (WinoGrande; Appendix~\\ref{app:winogrande}) to 0.45 (BoolQ; Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}), covering the full six-benchmark range within a single format. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm the ordering: all adjacent-benchmark CIs are non-overlapping except PIQA and HellaSwag, whose CIs overlap substantially ($[.127,\\,.159]$ vs.\\ $[.141,\\,.155]$; Appendix, Table~\\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \\citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}. The near-identical $\\alpha$ for PIQA (0.14) and HellaSwag (0.15) despite different evaluation formats suggests a shared reasoning-task floor for multiplicity, while retrieval (LAMBADA, 0.30) and mixed (ARC-Easy, 0.21) tasks produce distinctly higher values.",

```text
17	
18	Our analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent variation. The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval). Attention-driven aggregate convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0,\,-0.7]$, heuristic given $n = 3$, \S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}; cross-architecture $\sigma$ drops 79\%). LAMBADA \citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\rho| \leq 0.7$ and $p \geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is present at 1.3B but absent at 340M (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_convergence}). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\% attention (${\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. The experiments that follow begin with convergence (\S\ref{ssec:task_convergence}) because it establishes the aggregate backdrop against which per-example divergence (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}) and decoupling (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}) are measured.
19	
20	Our contributions are as follows:
21	\begin{itemize}
22	    \item We show that architectural variation---under matched training conditions---produces structured per-example predictive multiplicity with a task-dependent variation ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks in three recurrent backbones, with magnitude independent of aggregate convergence (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}, \S\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Unlike seed-driven disagreement within a single architecture \citep{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, this multiplicity is not addressable by seed averaging: the source of variation is the recurrent backbone rather than random initialization, though we cannot fully disentangle the two with single training runs (Limitations).
23	    \item We further observe that on LAMBADA, aggregate $\sigma$ decreases gradually with attention while pairwise Jaccard saturates at the first attention level, suggesting that attention reduces the performance gap through architecture-specific mechanisms rather than uniform rescue of the same examples (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}). This observation is dependent on GDN inclusion and does not replicate on other benchmarks.
24	\end{itemize}
25

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We present the first systematic per-example analysis of attention effects in hybrid recurrent models. Per-example multiplicity spans a task-dependent variation ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with Jaccard uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$) regardless of aggregate convergence. On LAMBADA, aggregate variance decreases with attention while per-example overlap saturates, suggesting architecture-specific mechanisms rather than uniform rescue; this observation is dependent on GDN inclusion and does not replicate on other benchmarks.
4	
5	For practitioners selecting among hybrid architectures, these results suggest a concrete protocol. Per-example audits are warranted whenever multiple hybrid designs achieve comparable aggregate scores on a target task: the low Jaccard ($\leq 0.10$, indicating ${\sim}90\%$ non-overlap) means that architectures which appear interchangeable by aggregate metrics affect nearly disjoint sets of examples. In deployment contexts where prediction consistency matters---medical, legal, or safety-critical applications---aggregate equivalence does not imply per-example equivalence, and architecture choice may determine which specific inputs are handled correctly. A proof-of-concept majority vote across three architectures does not outperform the best individual architecture (LAMBADA: $-0.2$\,pp; ARC-Easy: $-1.1$\,pp), despite 15--45\% per-example disagreement. Oracle accuracy---correct if any architecture is correct---reveals substantial complementarity potential ($+7$--$14$\,pp above best), but majority vote captures only 1--9\% of this ceiling. The failure of majority vote does not negate the value of per-example information: the oracle gap ($+7$--$14$\,pp) confirms that complementary knowledge exists across architectures, but accessing it requires selection methods more sophisticated than naive ensembling.
6

```text
72	\subsection{Decoupling}\label{ssec:decoupling}
73	
74	A key consequence of this multiplicity is that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence operate on different timescales as attention ratio increases (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}).
75	
76	Cross-architecture $\sigma$ decreases gradually across the five attention ratios: $0.024 \to 0.016 \to 0.014 \to 0.006 \to 0.005$. In contrast, pairwise Jaccard similarity between architectures' per-example effects saturates at the first attention level (4\%): approximately 0.095 for helped, with no further increase at higher ratios. Net asymmetry between architectures' per-example profiles increases from 0.013 at 4\% attention to 0.040 at 25\%. If convergence arose from attention uniformly rescuing the same difficult examples across architectures, Jaccard would increase in parallel with decreasing $\sigma$. The observed saturation of Jaccard at the first attention level indicates that each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\bar{J} \approx 0.10$ is far below the theoretical maximum $J_{\max} \approx 0.78$--$0.92$ given helped-set sizes, ruling out ceiling constraints: there is ample room for Jaccard to increase if attention were homogenizing per-example behavior (normalized effect size 5--7\% of achievable range; \S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
77	
78	Bootstrap confidence intervals (10{,}000 resamples) support this pattern statistically: the 95\% CI for $\sigma$ does not overlap between pure $[.019,\,.030]$ and h3-1 $[.002,\,.011]$, while the CIs for mean Jaccard overlap completely across all non-zero attention ratios (helped: $[.08,\,.11]$; hurt: $[.07,\,.11]$). Within-architecture Jaccard averages ${\sim}0.33$ (Appendix~\ref{app:within_arch_stability}), roughly $3\times$ the cross-architecture value (${\sim}0.10$). This $3\times$ gap compares within-architecture variation across attention ratios to cross-architecture variation at matched ratios---a proxy for, not a direct measurement of, seed-versus-architecture effects. Attributing the gap entirely to seed variation would require seeds to exceed the effect of replacing 25\% of model layers with attention---implausible though not directly testable with the available data.
79	
80	Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark variation (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}) confirms independence: LAMBADA ($\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing convergence despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\alpha = 0.15$) show no convergence with the lowest multiplicity. Cross-scale analysis on all four benchmarks reveals that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity is scale-robust: pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$ at 340M vs.\ $0.09$--$0.095$ at 1.3B), and architecture-specific helped/hurt profiles persist. While $\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M ($\Delta \leq 8$\,pp), this inflation does not correlate with accuracy loss across benchmarks (HellaSwag shows the largest accuracy gap yet one of the smallest $\alpha$ increases), suggesting capacity-related but not accuracy-driven modulation. At 340M, aggregate convergence is absent on all benchmarks ($\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$ for LAMBADA). This dissociation suggests that the \emph{pattern} of per-example multiplicity---which architectures affect which examples---reflects structural differences in recurrent processing, while absolute $\alpha$ magnitude is partially modulated by model capacity (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_per_example}).
81	
82	\subsection{Robustness}\label{ssec:robustness}
83	
84	Removing GDN weakens LAMBADA convergence from $\rho = -1.0$ to $\rho = -0.7$ ($p = .233$), partly because GDN's h3-1 uses 16 attention heads rather than 32 in all other configurations (Appendix~\ref{app:gdn_confound}). The RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves the decoupling: all hybrid $\sigma \leq 0.008$ while Jaccard saturates at 0.086--0.108. The aggregate convergence result is therefore dependent on GDN inclusion; the decoupling result is not. Full sensitivity analyses appear in Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}.
85	
```

</details>

      "content": "\\subsection{Decoupling}\\label{ssec:decoupling}\n\nA key consequence of this multiplicity is that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence operate on different timescales as attention ratio increases (Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}).\n\nCross-architecture $\\sigma$ decreases gradually across the five attention ratios: $0.024 \\to 0.016 \\to 0.014 \\to 0.006 \\to 0.005$. In contrast, pairwise Jaccard similarity between architectures' per-example effects saturates at the first attention level (4\\%): approximately 0.095 for helped, with no further increase at higher ratios. Net asymmetry between architectures' per-example profiles increases from 0.013 at 4\\% attention to 0.040 at 25\\%. If convergence arose from attention uniformly rescuing the same difficult examples across architectures, Jaccard would increase in parallel with decreasing $\\sigma$. The observed saturation of Jaccard at the first attention level indicates that each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\\bar{J} \\approx 0.10$ is far below the theoretical maximum $J_{\\max} \\approx 0.78$--$0.92$ given helped-set sizes, ruling out ceiling constraints: there is ample room for Jaccard to increase if attention were homogenizing per-example behavior (normalized effect size 5--7\\% of achievable range; \\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}).\n\nBootstrap confidence intervals (10{,}000 resamples) support this pattern statistically: the 95\\% CI for $\\sigma$ does not overlap between pure $[.019,\\,.030]$ and h3-1 $[.002,\\,.011]$, while the CIs for mean Jaccard overlap completely across all non-zero attention ratios (helped: $[.08,\\,.11]$; hurt: $[.07,\\,.11]$). This $3\\times$ gap compares within-architecture variation across attention ratios to cross-architecture variation at matched ratios---a proxy for, not a direct measurement of, seed-versus-architecture effects. ```text
24	\begin{abstract}
25	Hybrid linear recurrent architectures that interleave attention with recurrence are increasingly deployed, yet how attention affects per-example behavior across backbones remains unquantified. Using the controlled model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}---three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales (340M/1.3B), single training runs per configuration---we evaluate 10 benchmarks and decompose per-example effects on four (LAMBADA, HellaSwag, PIQA, ARC-Easy). Per-example predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} varies across tasks ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) while pairwise Jaccard of helped/hurt sets remains uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$); multiplicity magnitude depends on task type, not attention dose. Aggregate convergence, by contrast, is task-specific: only LAMBADA provides suggestive evidence for monotonic variance reduction (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$), consistent with attention compensating for retrieval differences across recurrent backbones. On LAMBADA, aggregate variance decreases gradually while per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level---the two are decoupled.
26	\end{abstract}
27
